\varrho(\xi)=c\cdot\xi

for all
\xi\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

4.

The weight
v
is continuous, submultiplicative,
and radially increasing.
Additionally,
\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}
and

\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad\text{defined by}\quad\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi):=\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi,\quad\text{for}\quad\xi\neq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(0):=c^{-1}

(8.1)

are
v
-moderate.

5.

There are constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
with

C_{0}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)\leq\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq C_{1}\cdot(1+\xi)\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)\qquad\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(8.2)

6.

We have

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}(\xi)|\leq v(\xi-\eta)\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)\qquad\forall\,\eta,\xi\in[0,\infty)\text{ and }\ell\in\underline{k+1}\,.

(8.3)

Note that the property (
8.3
) can equivalently be exchanged
by the simpler
|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}|\leq C\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}
, for all
\ell\in\underline{k+1}

(using that
\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}
is
v
-moderate and
v
is submultiplicative),
at the cost of introducing a multiplicative constant
Cv(0)

on the right-hand side of (
8.3
).

Remark 8.2
.

The reader may wonder why Definition
8.1
prescribes properties of
\varrho

on the negative half-axis at all.
These requirements are not strictly necessary, but neither are they an actual restriction:
The existence of an odd extension of regularity
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R})

of a function
\varrho_{0}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)

is, in fact, necessary for the radial warping function

\Phi_{\varrho_{0}}
induced by
\varrho_{0}
to be in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
This is easily seen by considering the case
d=1
.

On the other hand, the third condition in Definition
8.1

could indeed be slightly weakened, as long as
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)=\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi

has a positive, finite limit for
\xi\rightarrow 0

(and sufficiently many of its derivatives have a finite limit at
0
),
and none of the other conditions are violated.
However, the behavior of
\varrho
in a small neighborhood of zero has comparably little effect
on the induced warped time-frequency system.
The slow-start construction discussed in Section
8.2
provides a method
to modify functions satisfying a weaker variant of Definition
8.1

in a small neighborhood of zero, resulting in a
k
-admissible radial component.
Concerning the (lack of) impact of the slow-start construction on the resulting coorbit spaces,
cf. Remark
8.10
.

Remark 8.3
.

(1) An important consequence of these assumptions is that there exists a
constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varrho,v)>0
with

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}(\xi)|\leq C_{2}\cdot(1+\xi)\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)\qquad\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\text{ and }\ell\in\{0\}\cup\underline{k+1}\,.

(8.4)

Indeed, for
\ell=0

(
8.4
)
is always satisfied as long as
C_{2}\geq 1
, since
\varrho_{\ast}
is
increasing with
\varrho_{\ast}(0)=0
, whence

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(0)}(\xi)|=\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)=\xi\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)\leq(1+\xi)\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Thus, it remains to verify Equation
(
8.4
) for

\ell\in\underline{k+1}
.
But for this case, applying
(
8.3
) with
\eta=\xi
, we see
that

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}(\xi)|\leq v(\xi-\xi)\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)=v(0)\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\,,

so that (
8.2
) yields

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}(\xi)|\leq v(0)\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq C_{1}\cdot v(0)\cdot(1+\xi)\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)
.
Setting
C_{2}:=\max\{1,C_{1}\cdot v(0)\}
and
C_{1}
only depends on the radial component
\varrho
, we have thus
established (
8.4
).

(2) To indicate that being an admissible radial component is a nontrivial
restriction on
\varrho
, we observe that
condition (
8.2
) entails certain
growth restrictions on the function
\varrho_{\ast}=\varrho^{-1}
.
Indeed, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
and
\xi\geq 1/\varepsilon
, Equation
(
8.2
) shows

\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq C_{1}\cdot(1+\xi)\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi\leq(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)
. This implies

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle=-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\cdot e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)+e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)

\displaystyle\leq-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\cdot e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)+(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\cdot e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)=0

for all
\xi\geq 1/\varepsilon
. For any
\xi\geq a\geq 1/\varepsilon
, this implies

e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\leq e^{-(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}a}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(a)
,
and hence

\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\leq\frac{\varrho_{\ast}(a)}{e^{(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}a}}\cdot e^{(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}\xi}\qquad\forall\,\xi\geq a\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\,,\text{ for any }\varepsilon>0\,.

(8.5)

Likewise, the lower bound in (
8.2
)
implies

\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{-C_{0}}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\right)=(-C_{0})\xi^{-C_{0}-1}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)+\xi^{-C_{0}}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\geq(-C_{0})\xi^{-C_{0}}\cdot\frac{\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}+C_{0}\cdot\xi^{-C_{0}}\cdot\frac{\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}=0

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Thus, for
\xi\geq a>0
, we get

\xi^{-C_{0}}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\geq a^{-C_{0}}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}(a)
, and thus

\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)\geq\frac{\varrho_{\ast}(a)}{a^{C_{0}}}\cdot\xi^{C_{0}}\qquad\forall\,\xi\geq a>0\,.

(8.6)

In words, Equations (
8.5
)
and (
8.6
) show that

the inverse of an admissible radial component
\varrho

can grow at most exponentially, and has to grow at least
like a positive (not necessarily integer) power of
\xi
.

We define (for a larger class of radial components)
the radial warping function associated with
\varrho
.

Definition 8.4
.

For a diffeomorphism
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varrho(\xi)=c\xi
for all

\xi\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and suitable
\varepsilon,c>0
, the

associated radial warping function
is given by

\Phi_{\varrho}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\xi\mapsto\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\cdot\xi,\quad\text{with}\quad\widetilde{\varrho}(t):=\varrho(t)/t\quad\text{for }t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\varrho}(0):=c\,.

(8.7)

Clearly, if
\varrho\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
.
Our goal in this section is to show that
\Phi_{\varrho}
is a
k
-admissible
warping function as per Definition
4.2
,
provided that
\varrho
is a
k
-admissible radial component.
To this end, we first show that the inverse
\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}
of
\Phi_{\varrho}

is given by
\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}=\Phi_{\varrho^{-1}}
, and provide a convenient expression
of the Jacobian
\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}
.
The following notation
will be helpful for that purpose: For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, we define

\xi_{\circ}:=\xi/|\xi|,\qquad\pi_{\xi}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\tau\mapsto\langle\tau,\xi_{\circ}\rangle\cdot\xi_{\circ}\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{\xi}^{\perp}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\pi_{\xi}\,,

(8.8)

so that
\pi_{\xi}
is the orthogonal projection on the space spanned by
\xi
,
while
\pi_{\xi}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection on the orthogonal
complement of this space.
With these notations, the derivative of
\Phi_{\varrho}
and
\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}

can be described as follows:

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{k}
-diffeomorphism with
\varrho(t)=ct

for all
t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and suitable
\varepsilon,c>0
.
Then
\Phi_{\varrho}
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
, and for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)=\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\cdot\pi_{\xi}^{\perp}+\varrho^{\prime}(|\xi|)\cdot\pi_{\xi}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)]^{-1}=[\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)]^{-1}\cdot\pi_{\xi}^{\perp}+[\varrho^{\prime}(|\xi|)]^{-1}\cdot\pi_{\xi}\,.

(8.9)

Furthermore,
\Phi_{\varrho}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{k}
-diffeomorphism, with inverse

\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}=\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}
and
satisfies
\varrho_{\ast}(t)=t/c
for
t\in(-c\varepsilon,c\varepsilon)
.

Finally, if
\varrho
is a
0
-admissible radial component, then we have

\|[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)]^{-1}\|\lesssim 1/\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\xi|)\qquad\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\text{with }\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}\text{ as in }\eqref{eq:PsiTildeDefinition},

(8.10)

where the implied constant only depends on the constant

C_{0}
in (
8.2
).

Proof.

Recall that
\widetilde{\varrho}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
,
with
\widetilde{\varrho}\equiv c
on
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, and hence

\Phi_{\varrho}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Now, a direct computation using the identity

\partial_{j}|\xi|=\xi_{j}/|\xi|
shows for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}

that

\displaystyle\partial_{j}(\Phi_{\varrho})_{i}(\xi)=\partial_{j}\left(\xi_{i}\cdot\frac{\varrho(|\xi|)}{|\xi|}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\cdot\delta_{i,j}+\frac{\varrho^{\prime}(|\xi|)-\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)}{|\xi|^{2}}\cdot\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,.

In vector notation, and with
\xi_{\circ}=\xi/|\xi|
as in
(
8.8
), this means

\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)=\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\cdot\mathrm{id}+\left(\varrho^{\prime}(|\xi|)-\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\right)\cdot\xi_{\circ}\xi_{\circ}^{T}\,.

Now, recall that
\xi_{\circ}\xi_{\circ}^{T}
is the matrix representing the linear map

\pi_{\xi}
, and that
\mathrm{id}=\pi_{\xi}+\pi_{\xi}^{\perp}
.
Inserting these identities into the previous displayed equation establishes
the claimed formula for
\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)
. In particular, each
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

with
\eta\perp\xi
is mapped to
\widetilde{\varrho}(|\xi|)\cdot\eta
by

\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)
, while each
\eta\in\mathrm{span}(\xi)
is mapped to

\varrho^{\prime}(|\xi|)\cdot\eta
. Since
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\xi^{\perp}\oplus\mathrm{span}(\xi)
,
the stated formula for
[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)]^{-1}
follows.

Linearity of
\varrho_{\ast}(t)=t/c
for
t\in(-c\varepsilon,c\varepsilon)
is clear, such that

\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}
is a radial warping function as per Definition
8.4
.
Note
|\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)|=\varrho(|\xi|)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
such that
\varrho_{\ast}(|\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)|)=\varrho_{\ast}(\varrho(|\xi|))=|\xi|

and
\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)/|\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi)|=\xi/|\xi|
.
Together, this implies

\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\Phi_{\varrho}(\xi))=\xi\,,

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and thus, by continuity, for
\xi=0
as well.
Repeating this argument after interchanging
\varrho_{\ast}
and
\varrho

yields
\Phi_{\varrho}\circ\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}=\mathrm{id}
.

To prove (
8.10
), consider

\xi\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\vphantom{\sum_{j}}
and observe that

\|[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)]^{-1}\|=\max\big\{[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\xi|)]^{-1},[\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\xi|)]^{-1}\big\}
, by (
8.9
).
Applying the lower inequality in (
8.2
), we get

\|[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)]^{-1}\|\leq\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot 1/\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\xi|)\,.

For
\xi=0
the result follows by continuity.
∎

To verify Property (
4.5
) of
Definition
4.2
, i.e.,

\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}\left({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\right)\right\|\leq v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
,
for all
\tau,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\ \left|\alpha\right|\leq k
, we need to control certain
derivatives of the (matrix-valued) function

\phi_{\tau}\left({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\right)=\left(A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot A({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau)\right)^{T}\quad\text{with}\quad A(\tau)=\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}(\tau)

(8.11)

from (
4.4
). To this end, we will frequently
use
Faa di Bruno’s formula
, a chain rule for higher derivatives. Precisely, we will use the
following form of the formula, which is a slightly simplified (but less precise)
version of
[
28
, Corollary 2.10]
.
Note that, for a nonnegative multiindex
\alpha
, i.e.,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
, we denote the
sum of its components by
|\alpha|\geq 0
and by
\alpha=0
we refer to the unique
multiindex with
|\alpha|=0
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
n\in\underline{|\alpha|}
,
set

\Gamma_{\alpha,n}:=\left\{\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Big[\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\Big]^{n}\,:\,\smash{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\vphantom{\sum}\gamma_{j}=\alpha\right\}\,.

Furthermore, set

\Gamma:=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\bigcup_{n=1}^{|\alpha|}\Gamma_{\alpha,n}
.

Then, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there is a constant
D_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}

such that for any open sets
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}
, and any

\mathcal{C}^{k}
functions
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:U\to V
, the following holds for
any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\in\underline{k}
:

\partial^{\alpha}(f\circ g)(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{|\alpha|}\left[f^{(n)}(g(x))\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}}\bigg(D_{\gamma}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\partial^{\gamma_{j}}g)(x)\bigg)\right]\qquad\forall\,x\in U\,,

where
f^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-th derivative of
f
.

Remark
.

From the statement of
[
28
, Corollary 2.10]
,
it might appear that the constants
D_{\gamma}
also depend on
\alpha,n,d
,
in addition to
\gamma
. But these parameters are determined by
\gamma
:
On the one hand, we have
\gamma\in[\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}]^{n}
, which uniquely determines

n
and
d
. On the other hand,
\alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}
for

\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}
.

With these preparations, we can now prove that the radial warping function

\Phi_{\varrho}
associated to a
k
-admissible radial component
\varrho
is indeed
a
k
-admissible warping function.
Most significantly, the following proposition proves that Property (
4.5
),
cf. Definition
4.2
or the discussion preceding the above lemma,
is satisfied.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
k
-admissible radial
component with control weight
v:\mathbb{R}\!\to\!\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Then there is a constant
C\geq 1
, dependent on
\varrho
,
v
,
d
, and
k
,
such that with

v_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\tau\mapsto C\cdot(1+|\tau|)\cdot v(|\tau|),

the function
\Phi_{\varrho}
satisfies
(
4.5
) for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\leq k
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
v_{0}
is submultiplicative and radially increasing
as the product of submultiplicative and radially increasing weights

C
,
(1+|\bullet|)
and
v(|\bullet|)
.

The proof is divided into five steps.
As a preparation for these,
recall from
Lemma

8.5
that

\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}=\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}=(\bullet)\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\bullet|)
,
with
\varrho_{\ast}=\varrho^{-1}
and
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
as defined in
(
8.1
).
By
Lemma

8.5
,
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R})
.
Our main goal is to estimate the derivatives of
\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}
.

Step 1 - Estimate the derivatives of
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
:

A trivial induction shows

\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}t^{-1}=(-1)^{\ell}\cdot\ell!\cdot t^{-(1+\ell)}
.
With this, Leibniz’s rule shows for any
n\in\underline{k+1}

and any
t\in[c\varepsilon,\infty)
that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(t)-\frac{\varrho_{\ast}^{(n)}(t)}{t}\right|=\left|\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\binom{n}{\ell}\cdot\frac{d^{\ell}t^{-1}}{dt^{\ell}}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{(n-\ell)}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(k)\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}t^{-(1+\ell)}\cdot|\varrho_{\ast}^{(n-\ell)}(t)|

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:AdmissibilityHigherDerivativeConsequence}}{\leq}C(k)C_{2}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}t^{-\ell+1}\cdot\left(\frac{1+t}{t}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)}{1+t}

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{t^{-1}\leq(c\varepsilon)^{-1}\text{ and }(1+t)/t=t^{-1}+1\leq(c\varepsilon)^{-1}+1}})

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)/(1+t)\,,

(8.12)

where the constant
C(k)>0
in the first inequality only depends on
k
,
C_{2}
is as in
(
8.4
),
and
C^{(1)}
is given by
C^{(1)}=C(k)\cdot C_{2}((c\varepsilon)^{-1}+1)^{2}\cdot(k+1)\cdot\max\{1,(c\varepsilon)^{-k}\}
.

In particular,

|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(t)|\leq C^{(1)}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)/(1+t)+|\varrho_{\ast}^{(n)}(t)/t|,

such that

|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(t)|\leq C^{(1)}\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)}{1+t}+\left|\frac{\varrho_{\ast}^{(n)}(t)}{t}\right|\overset{\eqref{eq:AdmissibilityFirstDerivative},\eqref{eq:AdmissibilityHigherDerivative}}{\leq}(C^{(1)}C_{0}^{-1}+v(0))\cdot\frac{\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(t)}{t}.

(8.13)

Furthermore, the same estimate yields, with
(1+t)/t\leq(c\varepsilon)^{-1}+1

and (
8.4
),

|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(t)|\leq C^{(1)}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)+C_{2}\cdot((c\varepsilon)^{-1}+1)\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)\leq 2C^{(1)}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t),

(8.14)

where both (
8.13
)
and (
8.14
) hold for all
t\in[c\varepsilon,\infty)

and
n\in\underline{k+1}
.

Step 2 - Estimate the partial derivatives of
\tau\mapsto|\tau|
for
\tau\neq 0\,
:

It is well known that the derivative of order
n\in\mathbb{N}
of the square root function
t\mapsto t^{1/2}

has the form
t\mapsto c_{n}\cdot t^{-n+1/2}
, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and some constant
c_{n}\neq 0
.
Further, noting that
\partial^{\alpha}|\tau|^{2}=0
unless
\alpha=ie_{j}

for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
and
j\in\underline{d}
, it is easy to see that

\big|\,\partial^{\alpha}|\tau|^{2}\,\big|\leq 2\cdot|\tau|^{2-|\alpha|}

for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
.

Since
\tau\mapsto|\tau|
equals the composition
|\bullet|=(\bullet)^{1/2}\circ|\bullet|^{2}
,
Faa di Bruno’s formula (see Lemma
8.6
) yields

\big|\partial^{\alpha}|\tau|\big|=\left|\sum_{n=1}^{|\alpha|}c_{n}|\tau|^{1-2n}\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}}\bigg(D_{\gamma}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\bullet|^{2})(\tau)\bigg)\right|\,,

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
But we have
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}=\alpha
for

\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}
, and hence, using
n\leq|\alpha|
, we have
\left|\smash{\prod_{j=1}^{n}}\vphantom{\prod}(\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\bullet|^{2})(\tau)\right|\leq 2^{|\alpha|}\cdot|\tau|^{2n-|\alpha|}.

Overall, we obtain

\big|\partial^{\alpha}|\tau|\big|\leq C_{\alpha}\cdot|\tau|^{1-|\alpha|}\qquad\forall\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},

(8.15)

for some constants
C_{\alpha}
that may also depend on
d
.
The estimate is trivial in case of
\alpha=0
.

Step 3 - Estimate the partial derivatives of

\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R},\tau\mapsto\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)
:

Note that this map is just the composition of
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
with
the map
\tau\mapsto|\tau|
analyzed in the preceding step.
Thus, Faa di Bruno’s formula
(see Lemma
8.6
) shows for any

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
with
|\alpha|\leq k+1

and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
that

\partial^{\alpha}\zeta(\tau)=\sum_{n=1}^{|\alpha|}\left[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(|\tau|)\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}}\left(D_{\gamma}\cdot\smash{\prod_{j=1}^{n}}\vphantom{\prod}\,\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\tau|\right)\right]\,.

(8.16)

In the previous step, we saw
|\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\tau||\leq C_{\gamma_{j}}\cdot|\tau|^{1-|\gamma_{j}|}
.
Since
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}=\alpha
for

\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}
, there thus exists a
constant
C_{\gamma}>0
that may additionally depend on
d
, such that

\left|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\tau|\right|\leq C_{\gamma}\cdot|\tau|^{n-|\alpha|}\qquad\forall\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\quad n\in\underline{|\alpha|},\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}\,.

(8.17)

Now, let us focus on the case
|\tau|\geq c\varepsilon
.
Then, if
n\in\underline{|\alpha|-1}
, the estimate
(
8.14
) yields with

|\tau|^{n-|\alpha|}\leq(c\varepsilon)^{n+1-|\alpha|}\cdot|\tau|^{-1}\leq(c\varepsilon)^{n+1-|\alpha|}(1+(c\varepsilon)^{-1})/(1+|\tau|)

that

\left|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(n)}(|\tau|)\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha,n}}\!\!\!\left(D_{\gamma}\cdot\smash{\prod_{j=1}^{n}}\vphantom{\prod}\,\partial^{\gamma_{j}}|\tau|\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)}{1+|\tau|}\quad\forall\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{B_{c\varepsilon}}(0)\text{ and }n\in\underline{|\alpha|-1}\,.

(8.18)

Here,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\in\underline{k+1}
.

Overall, by combining (
8.16
)–(
8.18
)
(and noting that the case
n=|\alpha|
is not covered by (
8.18
)),
we get

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}\zeta(\tau)|=\Big|\partial^{\alpha}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big|\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}^{(|\alpha|)}(|\tau|)\Big|+\frac{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)}{1+|\tau|}

(8.19)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:PsiTildeDerivativeEstimateEasyCase}}{\lesssim}\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\quad\forall\,\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\,\text{ with }\,|\alpha|\in\underline{k+1}\,\text{ and }\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(0)\,.

(8.20)

We note that the total implied constant between the left and right hand sides
of Equation (
8.20
) depends on
\varrho,\ v
,
d
, and
k
.
Note that the quantities
c,\ \varepsilon,\ C_{0},\ C_{1}
and
C_{2}
that are more explicitly present
in the dependencies are themselves directly derived from
\varrho
and
v
.
Finally, the case
\alpha=0
is trivial.

Step 4 - Estimate

\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq k
and

{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)
:

Recall from (
8.11
) the definition of

\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})=\left(A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot A(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\right)^{T}
.
Since
\|M\|=\|M^{T}\|
for all
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
and since
\partial^{\alpha}[M(\tau)]^{T}=[\partial^{\alpha}M(\tau)]^{T}
for any
sufficiently smooth matrix-valued function
M:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
it is sufficient to estimate
\|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|

with
\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}):=A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot A(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
, where

A(\tau)=\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)
.

Furthermore,
\|M\|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}|M_{\bullet,j}|
for all
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
such that it is sufficient to estimate the columns of
M
individually.
In the following, we denote, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\partial^{\alpha}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}_{1}}\,\cdots\,\partial^{\alpha_{d}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}_{d}}}.

Let us fix
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then,
\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})=A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot(\partial^{\alpha}A)(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
.
We see that the
j
-th column of

\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
is simply

\left[\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\right]_{\bullet,j}=A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot\partial_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}^{\alpha}[A(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})]_{\bullet,j}=A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot(\partial_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}^{\alpha+e_{j}}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}})(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\,.

(8.21)

Now fix
j\in\underline{d}
, and set
\sigma:=\alpha+e_{j}
for brevity.
Note
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
with
|\sigma|\in\underline{k+1}
.

By definition of
\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}
, the
i
-th entry of

\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)
is

[\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)]_{i}=\tau_{i}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)
.
Let
\alpha_{+}
, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{d}
, be the elementwise positive part,
i.e.,
(\alpha_{+})_{i}=\max\{0,\alpha_{i}\}
,
i\in\underline{d}
.
The Leibniz rule, with
\partial^{\beta}\tau_{i}=0
for

\beta\notin\{0,e_{i}\}
and
\partial_{i}\tau_{i}=1
, yields

[\partial^{\sigma}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)]_{i}=\tau_{i}\cdot\big[\partial^{\sigma}(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|))\big]+\sigma_{i}\cdot\partial_{\tau}^{(\sigma-e_{i})_{+}}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big)\qquad\forall\,i\in\underline{d}\,,

or in other words,

\partial^{\sigma}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)=\big(\partial_{\tau}^{\sigma}\big[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big]\big)\cdot\tau+v_{\sigma,\tau}\,,\quad\text{with}\quad v_{\sigma,\tau}:=\left[\sigma_{i}\cdot\partial_{\tau}^{(\sigma-e_{i})_{+}}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big)\right]_{i=1,\dots,d}\,.

(8.22)

Now, by (
8.20
), we have

|v_{\sigma,\tau}|\lesssim\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)
,
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(0)
.
Furthermore, Lemma
8.5
provides the estimate

\|A^{-1}(\tau)\|=\|[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)]^{-1}\|\leq\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}/\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)

with
C_{0}
as in (
8.2
).
Note that we inserted the explicit constant derived
in the proof of Lemma
8.5
above.
Since
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
is
v
-moderate and
v
is radially
increasing, this implies

|A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot v_{\sigma,\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}}|\lesssim\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)}{\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)}\leq\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot v(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)=\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})

(8.23)

for all
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)
.
Thus, in view of (
8.22
),
it remains to estimate

\big(\partial_{(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})}^{\sigma}\big[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\big]\big)\cdot A^{-1}(\tau)\langle\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\rangle

for
\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(0)
.

Lemma
8.5
implies

A^{-1}(\tau)=[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)]^{-1}=[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)]^{-1}\cdot\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}+[\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)]^{-1}\cdot\pi_{\tau}\,.

(8.24)

Now, we apply (
8.20
),
and
v
-moderateness of
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
for radially increasing
v
, to derive

\begin{split}&\big|\partial_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}}^{\sigma}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\big)\big|\cdot\big[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big]^{-1}\cdot|\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})|\\
&\lesssim\big[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\big]^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\cdot|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|\\
&\leq v(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\cdot|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|\\
({\scriptstyle{v_{0}\geq(1+|\bullet|)\cdot v(|\bullet|)}})&\leq v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\,.\end{split}

(8.25)

Here, we additionally used the straightforward estimate

|\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})|=|\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}(\tau)+\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})|=|\pi_{\tau}^{\perp}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})|\leq|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|
.

Finally, with the elementary estimate
|\pi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau)|\leq|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|
, we get

\begin{split}&\big|\partial_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}}^{\sigma}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|)\big)\big|\cdot\left[\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)\right]^{-1}\cdot|\pi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau)|\\
({\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq:PsiTildeEuclideanTotalCase1},~\eqref{eq:AdmissibilityFirstDerivative},\text{ and }\eqref{eq:PsiTildeDerivativeEstimateEasyCase2}}})~&\lesssim|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|\cdot\frac{\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|)}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)}\\
({\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq:AdmissibilityHigherDerivative}}})~&\lesssim v(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\leq C^{(11)}\cdot v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\,.\end{split}

(8.26)

Overall, combining (
8.21
)–(
8.26
), we finally see

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|=\|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\max_{j\in\underline{d}}\big|\big[\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\big]_{\bullet,j}\big|

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq:RadialWarpingProductRulePreparation}}})~

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\max_{j\in\underline{d}}\big|A^{-1}(\tau)\cdot(\partial^{\alpha+e_{j}}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}})(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\big|

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq:PhiProductRuleApplication}}})~

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\max_{j\in\underline{d}}\left(\big|\partial_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}}^{\alpha+e_{j}}\big(\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau|)\big)\big|\cdot|A^{-1}(\tau)\,({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}+\tau)|+|A^{-1}(\tau)\,v_{\alpha+e_{j},\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}}|\right)

\displaystyle({\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq:RadialWarpingAdmissibleMainStepTerm1}\text{--}\eqref{eq:RadialWarpingAdmissibleMainStepTerm3}}})~

\displaystyle\lesssim v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\quad\text{for all }{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)\text{ and }|\alpha|\leq k\,,

where the implied constant between left and right hand side depends on
\varrho
,
v
,
d
, and
k
.

Step 5 - Estimate
\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})

for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq k
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)
:

By Lemma
8.5
,

\varrho_{\ast}(t)=t/c
, and thus
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(t)=c^{-1}
for

t\in(-c\varepsilon,c\varepsilon)
.
Hence,
\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)=c^{-1}\cdot\tau
for all

\tau\in B_{c\varepsilon}(0)
, so that

A(\tau)=\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)=c^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for

\tau\in B_{c\varepsilon}(0)
.

Hence,
\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})=A^{T}(\tau+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\cdot A^{-T}(\tau)=c^{-1}\cdot A^{-T}(\tau)
, whence

\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|=0\leq v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
for

{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with

|\alpha|\in\underline{k}
.
For
\alpha=0
, Eq. (
8.10
)
in
Lemma

8.5
shows

\displaystyle\|\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|=c^{-1}\cdot\|A^{-T}(\tau)\|=c^{-1}\cdot\|[\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)]^{-1}\|\leq\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot c^{-1}/\,\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\,.

But since
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
is
v
-moderate, we have

c^{-1}\!=\!\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(0)\leq\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)\cdot v(|\tau|)\,,

and finally
|\tau|\leq c\varepsilon+|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|
, such that

v(|\tau|)\leq v(c\varepsilon)\cdot v(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)
.
Altogether,
\|\phi_{\tau}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})\|\leq\max\{1,C_{0}^{-1}\}\cdot v(c\varepsilon)\cdot v(|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}|)\lesssim v_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}})
,
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{$\displaystyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\textstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptstyle\Upsilon$}}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon$}}}\in B_{c\varepsilon}(-\tau)
.
∎

That every radial warping function associated to a

k
-admissible radial component
\varrho
is indeed a
k
-admissible warping
function is now a straightforward corollary.

Corollary 8.8
.

Let
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
k
-admissible radial component with
control weight
v
, for some

k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq d+1
.
Then there is a constant
C\geq 1
, dependent on
\varrho
,
v
,
d
, and
k
,
such that with

v_{0}:\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad\tau\mapsto C\cdot(1+|\tau|)\cdot v(|\tau|),

the associated radial warping function

\Phi_{\varrho}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
k
-admissible warping function,
with control weight
v_{0}
. Furthermore, the weight
w=\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1})
is given by

w(\tau)=\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)\cdot[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)]^{d-1}\,.

(8.27)

Proof.

Lemma
8.5
shows that

\Phi_{\varrho}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
diffeomorphism
with
\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}=\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}
,
and (
8.9
) implies that

w(\tau)=\det\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\varrho_{\ast}}(\tau)=\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(|\tau|)\cdot[\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(|\tau|)]^{d-1}>0,
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
By continuity, and since
\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(0)=\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(0)=c^{-1}

is positive, the above formula remains true for
\tau=0
.
The remaining properties required in Definition
8.1

follow from Proposition
8.7
.
∎

8.2
The slow start construction for radial components

So far, see Definition
8.1
, we assumed that
a
k
-admissible radial component
\varrho
has to be linear on a neighborhood
of the origin.
Our goal in this section is to show that if a given function
\varsigma
satisfies
(slightly modified versions of) all the other conditions from
Definition
8.1
, then one can modify
\varsigma

in a neighborhood of the origin so that it becomes linear there, but
all other properties are retained.
We call this the
slow start construction
.

Definition 8.9
.

Fix some
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be continuous
and strictly increasing with
\varsigma(0)=0
.
Furthermore, fix

c\in\big(0,\varsigma(\varepsilon)/(2\varepsilon)\big)
,
and an even function
\Omega\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies

\Omega(\xi)=1
for
x\in B_{\varepsilon}(0)
,
\Omega(\xi)=0
for
x\not\in B_{2\varepsilon}(0)
,
and
\Omega^{\prime}(\xi)\leq 0
for
\xi\in[0,\infty)
.
Then the function

\varrho:\quad\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\xi\mapsto\begin{cases}c\xi\cdot\Omega(\xi)+\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot(1-\Omega(\xi))\cdot\varsigma(|\xi|),&\text{if }\xi\neq 0\,,\\
0,&\text{if }\xi=0\end{cases}

(8.28)

is called a
slow start version
of
\varsigma
.

Remark 8.10
.

The intent of the slow start construction is to establish a
k
-admissible warping function
that only differs from a radial function derived directly from
\varsigma

in a small neighborhood of zero.
This raises the question whether different slow start versions of
\varsigma
,
obtained, e.g., by choosing different values of
\varepsilon
in
Definition

8.9
,
are equivalent in the sense that they generate the same coorbit spaces.
Although we suspect that this can be shown directly
by verifying the conditions of Proposition
2.15
,
instead, under fairly general conditions, we will obtain this equivalence
as a consequence of identifying the respective coorbit spaces
with certain decomposition spaces
[
42
,
19
,
94
,
95
]

in a follow-up contribution.

The following lemma summarizes the main
elementary
properties of this
construction.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be continuous and strictly
increasing with
\varsigma(0)=0
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary, and
c\in\big(0,\varsigma(\varepsilon)/(2\varepsilon)\big)
.
Then, the function
\varrho
defined in (
8.28
)
has the following properties:

1.

We have
\varrho(\xi)=\varsigma(\xi)

for all
\xi\in[2\varepsilon,\infty)
.

2.

\varrho
is antisymmetric.

3.

\varrho(\xi)=c\xi
for all

\xi\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

4.

If
\varsigma|_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}

for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then
\varrho
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
.

5.

If
\varsigma|_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}
is
C^{1}

with
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0
for all
\xi\in(\varepsilon,\infty)
,
then
\varrho^{\prime}(\xi)>0
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.

6.

If
\varsigma|_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
with
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0
for all

\xi\in(\varepsilon,\infty)
, and if furthermore

\varsigma(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
,
then
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{k}
-diffeomorphism and

\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a homeomorphism.
Finally, we have

\varrho^{-1}(\xi)=\varsigma^{-1}(\xi)\qquad\forall\,\xi\in[\varsigma(2\varepsilon),\infty)\,.

Remark
.

Item (
6
) above is particularly interesting, since it is
often more important to know the properties of the

inverse
of the warping function (
\Phi_{\varrho}^{-1}=\Phi_{\varrho^{-1}}
by
Lemma
8.5
) than those of the warping function itself.

Proof.

Ad (
1
):
For
\xi\in[2\varepsilon,\infty)
,
we have
\Omega(\xi)=0
.
Therefore,
\varrho(\xi)=\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot\varsigma(|\xi|)=\varsigma(\xi)
.

Ad (
2
):

\Omega
is symmetric, i.e.,
\Omega(-\xi)=\Omega(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.
For
\xi\neq 0
, this implies

\displaystyle\varrho(-\xi)

\displaystyle=c\cdot(-\xi)\cdot\Omega(-\xi)+\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(-\xi)\cdot(1-\Omega(-\xi))\cdot\varsigma(|-\xi|)

\displaystyle=-\Big(c\xi\cdot\Omega(\xi)+\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot(1-\Omega(\xi))\cdot\varsigma(|\xi|)\Big)=-\varrho(\xi)\,.

For
\xi=0
, we trivially have
\varrho(-\xi)=0=-\varrho(\xi)
.

Ad (
3
):

By choice of
\Omega
, we have
\Omega(\xi)=1
for
\xi\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.
For
\xi\neq 0
, this immediately yields

\varrho(\xi)=c\xi
, which clearly also holds for
\xi=0
.

Ad (
4
):
Since
\Omega
is smooth, and since
the functions
\xi\mapsto\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)
and
\xi\mapsto|\xi|
are smooth on

\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, it is clear that
\varrho
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
on

\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. But in the preceding point we saw that
\varrho
is
linear (and hence smooth) in a neighborhood of zero.
Hence,
\varrho
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
.

Ad (
5
):
On
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, we have

\varrho(\xi)\!=\!c\xi
, and thus
\varrho^{\prime}(\xi)\!=\!c\!>\!0
on
[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
.
Also, on
(-\infty,-2\varepsilon)\cup(2\varepsilon,\infty)
, we have
\Omega(\xi)=0
,
and hence
\varrho(\xi)=\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot\varsigma(|\xi|)
.
Since
\xi\mapsto|\xi|
is smooth away from zero, with

\frac{d}{d\xi}|\xi|=\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)
, this implies

\varrho^{\prime}(\xi)=(\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi))^{2}\cdot\varsigma^{\prime}(|\xi|)>0
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
with

|\xi|\geq 2\varepsilon
.

For
\xi\in(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)
, we have

\varrho^{\prime}(\xi)=\left[\Omega(\xi)\cdot c+(1-\Omega(\xi))\cdot\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)\right]+(-\Omega^{\prime}(\xi))\cdot(\varsigma(\xi)-c\xi)>0,

since all three terms are nonnegative and they cannot vanish simultaneously.
To see this, note that
\Omega^{\prime}(\xi)\leq 0
for
\xi\in[0,\infty)
,
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0
for
\xi\in(\varepsilon,\infty)
,
and
\varsigma(\xi)\geq\varsigma(\varepsilon)>2c\varepsilon>c\xi
for
\xi\in(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)
.
For the last inequality, recall
c\in(0,\varsigma(\varepsilon)/(2\varepsilon))
.
Positivity of
\varrho^{\prime}
on
(-2\varepsilon,-\varepsilon)
follows from
\varrho
being antisymmetric.

Ad (
6
):

We have
\varrho(0)=0
and
\varrho(\xi)=\varsigma(\xi)
for
\xi\geq 2\varepsilon
, such that

\varrho([0,\infty))\supset[0,\infty)
by the intermediate value theorem.
Hence,
\varrho
is surjective by (
2
) and with
\varrho^{\prime}>0
by
(
5
) even bijective.
As a strictly increasing bijective
\mathcal{C}^{k}
map with positive derivative,

\varrho
is a
\mathcal{C}^{k}
-diffeomorphism by the inverse function theorem.

Similar arguments show that
\varsigma
is a homeomorphism.
The remaining property
\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)=\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in[\varsigma(2\varepsilon),\infty)

is now a straightforward consequence of
\varrho(\xi)=\varsigma(\xi)
for all
\xi\in[2\varepsilon,\infty)
.
∎

Our final goal in this subsection is to state convenient criteria on
\varsigma

which ensure that
\varrho
is a
k
-admissible radial component.
For this, the following general lemma will be helpful.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
\delta>0
, and let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[\delta,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

and
u:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be continuous and increasing
with
u(\xi+\eta)\leq u(\xi)\cdot u(\eta)
for all
\xi,\eta\in[0,\infty)
.
Furthermore, assume that there is some
D>0
such that

D\leq\theta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(\eta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\theta_{1}(\xi)\leq\theta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\qquad\forall\,\xi,\eta\in[\delta,\infty)\,.

(8.29)

If
\beta_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and
\beta_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
are
continuous with
\beta_{j}(\xi)=\theta_{j}(|\xi|)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
with

|\xi|\geq\delta
and all
j\in\{1,2\}
, then there is a constant

C\geq 1
with

\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq C\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\qquad\forall\,\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}\,.

Proof.

By continuity of
\beta_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and
\beta_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
with
\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq c_{1}
and

\beta_{2}(\xi)\geq c_{2}
for all
\xi\in[-\delta,\delta]
.
Further, note that the conditions on
u
imply
u(0)\geq 1

and that
u(|\bullet|)
is submultiplicative and radially increasing.
We distinguish four cases:

Case 1 (
|\xi|<\delta
and
|\eta|<\delta
):

\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq c_{1}\leq\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}\cdot u(0)}\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\,.

Case 2 (
|\xi|\geq\delta
and
|\eta|\geq\delta
):

\beta_{1}(\xi)=\theta_{1}(|\xi|)\leq\theta_{2}(|\eta|)\cdot u\big(\big|\,|\xi|-|\eta|\,\big|\big)\leq\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\,.

Case 3 (
|\xi|<\delta
and
|\eta|\geq\delta
):
We have

D\leq\theta_{2}(|\eta|)\cdot u(|\eta|)\leq\theta_{2}(|\eta|)\cdot u(|\eta-\xi|)\cdot u(\delta)\,,

since
u(|\xi|)\leq u(\delta)
.
Hence,

\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq c_{1}\leq\frac{c_{1}\cdot u(\delta)}{D}\cdot\theta_{2}(|\eta|)\cdot u(|\eta-\xi|)\leq\frac{c_{1}\cdot u(\delta)}{D}\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\eta-\xi|)\,.

Case 4 (
|\xi|\geq\delta
and
|\eta|<\delta
):
We have

\big|\,|\xi|-\delta\,\big|\leq|\xi|\leq|\xi-\eta|+|\eta|<|\xi-\eta|+\delta
.
Hence,

\beta_{1}(\xi)=\theta_{1}(|\xi|)\leq c_{2}^{-1}\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot\theta_{1}(|\xi|)\leq\frac{\theta_{2}(\delta)\cdot u(\delta)}{c_{2}}\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\,.

Altogether, we have shown
\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq C\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)

for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, with

C:=\max\left\{1,\quad\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}\cdot u(0)},\quad\frac{c_{1}\cdot u(\delta)}{D},\quad\frac{\theta_{2}(\delta)\cdot u(\delta)}{c_{2}}\right\}\,.\qed

We now formally introduce a class of functions

\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
for which the slow-start construction
produces a
k
-admissible radial component.
This will be proven in Proposition
8.15
below.

Definition 8.13
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
A continuous function
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

is called a
weakly
k
-admissible radial component with control weight

u:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, if it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

\varsigma
is
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, with
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

2.

\varsigma(0)=0
and
\varsigma(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
.

3.

The control weight
u
is continuous and increasing with

u(\xi+\eta)\leq u(\xi)\cdot u(\eta)
for all
\xi,\eta\in[0,\infty)
.
Furthermore, there are
\delta>0
and
C_{0},C_{1}>0
with the following properties:

\displaystyle C_{0}\cdot\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}\leq\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq C_{1}\cdot\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad\forall\,\xi\in[\delta,\infty)\,,

(8.30)

\displaystyle\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}\leq\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\eta)}{\eta}\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)

\displaystyle\qquad\forall\,\xi,\eta\in[\delta,\infty)\,,

(8.31)

\displaystyle|\varsigma_{\ast}^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)

\displaystyle\qquad\forall\,\xi,\eta\in[\delta,\infty)\text{ and }m\in\underline{k+1}\,.

(8.32)

Remark 8.14
.

Properties (1) and (2) imply that
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a homeomorphism, with
inverse
\varsigma_{\ast}:=\varsigma^{-1}
.

In many cases, one even has the stronger condition
\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\asymp\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi

for all
\xi\in[\delta,\infty)
instead of (
8.30
).
In this case, it is not necessary
to verify condition (
8.31
), since—after
possibly replacing
u
by
C\cdot u
for some
C\geq 1
—this condition
is implied by (
8.32
) for
m=1
.
Indeed, if (
8.32
) holds, then

\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}\asymp\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\lesssim\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\eta)}{\eta}\cdot u(|\xi-\eta|)\qquad\text{for }\xi,\eta\in[\delta,\infty)\,.

Overall, if
\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\asymp\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi

for
\xi\in[\delta,\infty)
, then
\varsigma
is a weakly
k
-admissible radial component, if
\varsigma
is
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
with
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0
,

\varsigma(0)=0
and with
\varsigma(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
and
\varsigma
satisfies (
8.32
).

Our final result in this subsection shows that the slow-start construction,
applied to a weakly
k
-admissible radial component, yields a
k
-admissible
radial component.

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and let
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a
weakly
k
-admissible radial component with control weight

u:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Furthermore, let
\varrho
be a “slow-start version” of
\varsigma

as in (
8.28
).
Then there exists a constant
C:=C(k)\geq 1
, such that
\varrho
is a
k
-admissible radial component
with control weight

v:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi\mapsto C\cdot u(|\xi|)\,.

Proof.

Lemma
8.11
shows that
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

satisfies conditions (1)–(3) of Definition
8.1
.
As already observed in the proof of Lemma
8.11
,
the conditions on
u
imply that
u(|\bullet|)
is submultiplicative,
such that the same holds for
v
, since
C\geq 1
.
Note furthermore, that
\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)=\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)

for all
\xi\geq\delta^{\prime}:=\max\{\varsigma(2\varepsilon),\delta\}
,
with
\varepsilon>0
as in Lemma
8.11

and
\delta>0
as in Definition
8.13
.

We proceed to prove condition (5) of Definition
8.1
:
For
|\xi|\geq\delta^{\prime}
, the inequality (
8.2
)
(with some constants
\tilde{C}_{1},\tilde{C}_{2}
in place of
C_{1},C_{2}
)
is a direct consequence of (
8.30
) and (
8.28
).

For
|\xi|\leq\varsigma(\varepsilon)
,
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)=\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)/\xi=c^{-1}
,
such that
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
is continuous and there are
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}>0
,
such that for all
\xi\in[-\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}]
,
c_{1}\leq\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}(\xi)\leq c_{2}

and
c_{3}\leq\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq c_{4}
.
Thus, with
C_{1}=\min\{\tilde{C}_{1},c_{3}/c_{2}\}

and
C_{2}=\max\{\tilde{C}_{2},c_{4}/c_{1}\}
, (
8.2
) is satisfied
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.

To prove condition (6) of Definition
8.1
, consider the following:
For
|\xi|\geq\delta^{\prime}
, the antisymmetry of
\varrho
implies that

\varrho_{\ast}(\xi)=\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot\varsigma_{\ast}(\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot\xi)
.
A straightforward induction therefore shows

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(m)}(\xi)|=\big|\varsigma_{\ast}^{(m)}(\mathop{\operatorname{sgn}}(\xi)\cdot\xi)\big|=\big|\varsigma_{\ast}^{(m)}(|\xi|)\big|\quad\text{for all}\quad m\in\underline{k+1}\quad\text{and}\quad|\xi|\geq\delta^{\prime}.

Furthermore, note that (
8.32
) with
m=1

and
\xi=\delta
and
\varsigma^{\prime}(\xi)>0
for all
\xi\in(\varepsilon,\infty)

implies
0<\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\delta)/u(\delta)\leq\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)u(\eta)
, since
u
is increasing.

Fix some
\ell\in\underline{k+1}
.
In view of (
8.32
),
we can apply Lemma
8.12
(with
\delta^{\prime}
instead of

\delta
), with
\theta_{1}=\big|\varsigma_{\ast}^{(\ell)}|_{[\delta^{\prime},\infty)}\,\big|
,

\theta_{2}=\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}|_{[\delta^{\prime},\infty)}
,
and with
\beta_{1}=|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}|
,
\beta_{2}=\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}
.
Consequently, there is a constant
G_{\ell}\geq 1
such that

|\varrho_{\ast}^{(\ell)}(\xi)|=\beta_{1}(\xi)\leq G_{\ell}\cdot\beta_{2}(\eta)\cdot u(|\eta-\xi|)=G_{\ell}\cdot\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)\cdot u(|\eta-\xi|)\qquad\forall\,\eta,\xi\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(8.33)

Since
\ell\in\underline{k+1}
was arbitrary, (
8.3
) is satisfied
with
C\geq\max\{G_{1},\dots,G_{k+1}\}
.

In particular, if we set
\ell=1
, then (
8.33
) implies
that
\varrho_{\ast}^{\prime}
is
v
-moderate with
v=Cu(|\bullet|)
and any
C\geq G_{1}
.
Hence, for condition (4) in Definition
8.1
it only remains
to prove that
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
is
v
-moderate.

With
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=\varsigma_{\ast}/|\bullet|
(and
\delta^{\prime}
instead of
\delta
)
the inequality (
8.29
)
is implied by (
8.31
).
Therefore, we can invoke Lemma
8.12

with this choice of
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
and
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
.
Note that
\beta_{j}(\xi)=\theta_{j}(|\xi|)
for
|\xi|\geq\delta^{\prime}
.
We obtain a constant
G\geq 1
, such that
\widetilde{\varrho_{\ast}}
is
v
-moderate
with
v=Cu(|\bullet|)
and any
C\geq G
.
Altogether, condition (4) in Definition
8.1
is satisfied
with
v=Cu(|\bullet|)
, for any
C\geq\max\{G_{1},G\}
.
∎

8.3
Examples of radial warping functions

We now present two examples of radial
components
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
.
We show that they are
weakly
k
-admissible

as per Definition
8.13
.
By Proposition
8.15

and Corollary
8.8
,
any slow start version
\varrho
of
\varsigma
yields a radial,

k
-admissible warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}
.
Additionally, we provide in each case a control weight
v_{0}
for
\Phi_{\varrho}
.

Example 8.16
.

Let
p\geq 1
, and consider the function

\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty),\xi\mapsto(1+\xi)^{1/p}-1\,.

Conditions (1)–(2) of Definition
8.13
are clear. For
p=1
, Condition (3) is easily verified with
u\equiv 1
.
To verify Condition (3) for
p>1
, we first show that

\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}\asymp\varsigma_{\ast}/(\bullet).

By Remark
8.14
, it is then sufficient to verify only
(
8.32
).

Note that
\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)=(1+\xi)^{p}-1
.
For
\xi>\delta:=1
, it is easy to see that
(1+\xi)^{r}-1\asymp(1+\xi)^{r}
, for any
r>0
.
In particular, with
r=p-1
, we obtain

\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\xi)=p\cdot(1+\xi)^{p-1}\asymp\frac{(1+\xi)^{p}}{1+\xi}\asymp\frac{(1+\xi)^{p}-1}{\xi}=\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}\quad\text{for}\quad\xi\geq 1\,.

Note the inequality

1+\xi\leq 1+\eta+|\xi-\eta|\leq(1+\eta)\cdot(1+|\xi-\eta|)
,
which holds for
\eta,\xi\geq 0
.
As a direct consequence, we obtain for all
\eta,\xi\geq 0
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}

with
\alpha\leq\beta
that

(1+\xi)^{\alpha}\leq(1+\xi)^{\beta}\leq(1+\eta)^{\beta}\cdot(1+|\eta-\xi|)^{|\beta|}\,.

(8.34)

Define
\tilde{u}=(1+(\bullet))^{|p-1|}
and note that

\varsigma_{\ast}^{(m)}(\xi)=C_{m}\cdot(1+\xi)^{p-m}
for all
m\in\underline{k+1}
,
for suitable constants
C_{m}=C_{m}(m,p)\in\mathbb{R}
, in particular,
C_{1}=p>0
.
Therefore,

|\varsigma_{\ast}^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq|C_{m}|\cdot(1+\xi)^{p-m}\overset{\eqref{eq:PolynomialWeightModerateness}}{\leq}|C_{m}|\cdot(1+\eta)^{p-1}\tilde{u}(|\xi-\eta|)=\frac{|C_{m}|}{p}\cdot\varsigma_{\ast}^{\prime}(\eta)\cdot\tilde{u}(|\xi-\eta|)\,,

for all
\eta,\xi\geq 1
.
This proves (
8.32
)
with
u=\max_{m\in\underline{k+1}}\{|C_{m}|/p\}\cdot\tilde{u}
.

Hence,
\varsigma
is a weakly
k
-admissible radial component with control weight

u:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
u(\xi)=C\cdot(1+\xi)^{|p-1|}
,
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and some appropriate constant
C\geq 1
, depending on
p
and
k
.
By Proposition
8.15
any “slow start” version
\varrho

of
\varsigma
is
k
-admissible, with control weight
v=C^{\prime}\cdot u(|\bullet|)
, for some

C^{\prime}\geq 1
. Therefore, Corollary
8.8
shows that the associated
radial warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}
is indeed a
k
-admissible warping function
with control weight
v_{0}=C^{\prime\prime}\cdot(1+|\bullet|)\cdot u(|\bullet|)=C^{\prime\prime}(1+|\bullet|)^{1+|p-1|}
,
for constant
C^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
.

At this point, we conjecture that the coorbit spaces

\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi_{\varrho}),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})

that are associated to the warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}
constructed here
coincide with certain
\alpha
-modulation spaces
, specifically with
\alpha=p^{-1}(p-1)\in[0,1)
,
for a proper choice of the weight
\kappa
.
In future work, we will verify this by identifying

\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi_{\varrho}),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
with
certain decomposition spaces, cf.
[
19
,
42
]
, and considering embeddings
between the resulting decomposition spaces and

\alpha
-modulation spaces

[
51
,
41
,
55
,
30
]

using the theory developed in
[
94
,
95
]
.

Example 8.17
.

Consider the function
\varsigma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty),\xi\mapsto\ln(1+\xi)
.
It is easy to see that conditions (1)–(2) of Definition
8.13

are satisfied and that
\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)=\varsigma^{-1}(\xi)=e^{\xi}-1
.

We now verify condition (3) of Definition
8.13

by proving that the inequalities (
8.30
)–
(
8.32
) hold with
\delta=1
and

u:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty),\xi\mapsto e^{\xi}
.
Note that
\varsigma_{\ast}^{(\ell)}=u
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that (
8.32
) clearly holds,
even for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Ad (
8.30
):
For
\xi\geq\delta=1
, we
have
1\leq e^{\xi}/e
, and thus

\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)=e^{\xi}-1\geq e^{\xi}\cdot(1-e^{-1})
. Therefore,

\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}\leq e^{\xi}\leq(1-e^{-1})^{-1}\cdot\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)\,,

so that (
8.30
) is fulfilled with

C_{0}=1
and
C_{1}=(1-e^{-1})^{-1}>0
.

Ad (
8.31
):
Let

\widetilde{\varsigma_{\ast}}(\xi):=\frac{\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)}{\xi}=\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}
for

\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and note that
\widetilde{\varsigma_{\ast}}
has the power series
expansion

\widetilde{\varsigma_{\ast}}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\xi}\cdot\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{n}}{n!}-1\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{n-1}}{n!}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{\ell}}{(\ell+1)!},

which shows that
\widetilde{\varsigma_{\ast}}
is increasing, since each term
of the series is increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Therefore,
\xi\leq\eta
implies

\varsigma_{\ast}(\xi)\leq\varsigma_{\ast}(\eta)\leq\varsigma_{\ast}(\eta)e^{|\xi-\eta|}
.

If
0<\eta<\xi
, then

\frac{e^{\eta}-1}{\eta}\cdot e^{|\xi-\eta|}=\frac{e^{\eta}-1}{\eta}\cdot e^{\xi-\eta}=\frac{1-e^{-\eta}}{\eta}\cdot e^{\xi}\geq\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}\,.

Here, the final inequality uses that
\xi\mapsto\tfrac{1-e^{-\xi}}{\xi}
is decreasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Therefore, (
8.31
) even holds for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

In other words,
\varsigma
is a weakly

k
-admissible radial component with control weight

u:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
u(\xi)=e^{\xi}
(for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
).
By
Proposition

8.15
, any “slow start version”
\varrho

of
\varsigma
as per (
8.28
), is a
k
-admissible
radial component with control weight
v:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\xi\mapsto C\cdot e^{|\xi|}
,
for some
C\geq 1
. By
Corollary

8.8
, the associated radial warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}

is a
k
-admissible warping function with control weight

v_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\tau\mapsto C^{\prime}\cdot(1+|\tau|)\cdot e^{|\tau|}
,
for a suitable
C^{\prime}\geq 1
.

It is likely that the coorbit spaces

\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi_{\varrho}),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
associated
with the warping function
\Phi_{\varrho}
constructed can be embedded into certain

inhomogeneous Besov spaces

[
88
,
90
,
89
]
,
if the weight
\kappa
is chosen properly.
If such an embedding exists, we expect the converse to be true as well,
possibly with a different weight
\tilde{\kappa}
instead of
\kappa
.
Similar to the previous examples, the interpretation of

\operatorname{Co}(\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi_{\varrho}),\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa})
as decomposition
space will be the first step towards verifying such embeddings.

9
Conclusion

We developed a theory of warped time-frequency systems for functions of arbitrary dimensionality.
These systems, defined by a prototype function
\theta
and a diffeomorphism
\Phi
,
form tight continuous frames and admit the construction of coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)
,
which we have shown to be well-defined Banach spaces, provided that

\Phi
is a
k
-admissible warping function and
Y
is a suitable, solid Banach space.
We have further shown that stable discretization, in the sense of Banach frame decompositions,
of the continuous system
\mathcal{G}(\theta,\Phi)
is achieved across said coorbit spaces,
simply by sampling densely enough.
In all cases, the results are realized by choosing the prototype
\theta
from a class of smooth,
localized functions that includes
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Moreover, the results can be invoked simultaneously for a large class
of spaces
Y
including, but not limited to, weighted mixed-norm Lebesgue spaces

\mathbf{L}^{p,q}_{\kappa}
,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.
Finally, we considered radial warping functions as an important special case,
showed how they can be constructed from (weakly) admissible radial components,
and provided examples of radial warping functions for which we expect a relation
to well-known smoothness spaces.
Altogether, we have demonstrated that warped time-frequency systems,
a vast class of translation-invariant time-frequency systems that enable the adaptation to a
specific frequency-bandwidth relationship, can be analyzed with a unified,
and surprisingly deep mathematical theory.

There is an abundance of opportunities for further generalization,
of which we mention only two:
(1) That the weight
m
may only depend on the time variable
if
\sup_{\xi\in D}\|D\Phi(\xi)\|<\infty

(in
Theorems

4.4
and
6.1
)
remains an irritating and somewhat unnatural condition,
but cannot be dropped if
m
is to be majorized by the product of a time-dependent
and another frequency-dependent weight.
If the latter requirement is relaxed and a more general weight is considered,
it may be possible to consider time-dependent weights if
\|D\Phi(\xi)\|
is unbounded.
(2) The construction analyzed in this work does not accommodate frames
with arbitrary directional sensitivity.
In particular, the degree of anisotropy is determined directly by the warping function
and cannot be chosen freely.
For example, without further modification,
it cannot currently mimic popular directional frames like curvelets or shearlets,
or even isotropic wavelets; see below for the last point.

While the present article shows that the coorbit spaces
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)

are well-defined Banach spaces admitting a rich discretization theory,
it does not answer all open questions regarding the structure of
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)

as
smoothness spaces
.
These questions concern, e.g., the description of
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)
purely in terms
of Fourier analysis, as well as the existence of embeddings between the spaces
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)

for different choices of the warping function
\Phi
and the space
Y
,
or between
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)
and established smoothness spaces, such as Besov spaces,
Sobolev spaces,
\alpha
-modulation spaces, or spaces of dominating mixed smoothness

[
74
,
75
,
96
]
.
In a follow-up article, we will study these questions
in the context of decomposition spaces,
a common generalization of Besov- and modulation spaces.
Specifically, we will show that the spaces
\operatorname{Co}_{\Phi}(Y)
are special decomposition spaces,
so that the rich theory of these spaces can be employed to answer the questions posed above.
In that work, we will confirm the conjectured relation to
\alpha
-modulation spaces
(see Example
8.16
) and prove that equality
between (inhomogeneous) Besov spaces and the coorbit spaces related to warped time-frequency systems
can only be achieved in the one-dimensional case,
thereby making the statement about isotropic wavelets in the previous paragraph formal.

Appendix A
Formal details for making sense of the intersection
B\cap B^{\prime}

To make sense of the intersection
B\cap B^{\prime}
that appears in
Definition

2.1
,
we assume that
B
is compatible with a suitable
Gelfand triple

in the following sense:
We assume that there exists a topological vector space
V
of ”test functions”
which satisfies
V\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
, with dense image.
For instance, in the case
\mathcal{H}=\mathbf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
one could choose
V=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

or
V=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
One can then identify each
h\in\mathcal{H}
with the anti-linear functional
(or ”generalized distribution”)

\varphi_{h}:\quad V\to\mathbb{C},\quad v\mapsto\langle h,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

since it is easy to see that the map
\mathcal{H}\to V^{\urcorner},h\mapsto\varphi_{h}

is linear and injective.
Since
V\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
, we can thus consider
V
as a subset of
V^{\urcorner}
,
by virtue of the dual pairing coming from
\mathcal{H}
.

Then, we say that a Banach space
B
is compatible with the Gelfand triple
(V,\mathcal{H},V^{\urcorner})
,
if
B
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

(B,\|\cdot\|_{B})
is a Banach space,

(ii)

B\subset V^{\urcorner}
as sets (here, one potentially has to make some (canonical) identifications,
such as considering
\mathbf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as a subset of
\mathcal{S}^{\urcorner}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
),

(iii)

the inclusion
B\hookrightarrow V^{\urcorner}
is continuous
(with respect to the weak-
\ast
-topology on
V^{\urcorner}
),

(iv)

V\subseteq B
is dense
(this rules out spaces such as
B=\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
\mathcal{H}=\mathbf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
V=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, but one can then instead use the closure of
V
in
B
,
which in this case would be equal to
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
the space of continuous functions ”vanishing at infinity”).

For such a compatible Banach space, we then say that
b\in B\subset V^{\urcorner}

satisfies
b\in B\cap B^{\prime}
, if there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|b(v)|\leq C\cdot\|v\|_{B}\qquad\forall\,v\in V.

Since
V\subset B
is dense, this implies that
\overline{b}\in V^{\prime}

(given by
\langle\overline{b},v\rangle_{V,V^{\prime}}=\overline{b(v)}
)
uniquely extends to a continuous linear functional on
B
;
we then identify
b
with this functional.

Note that if
h\in B\cap\mathcal{H}
, then since we are identifying
h
with the functional
\varphi_{h}
,
we have for
v\in V
that

\overline{h}(v)=\overline{\varphi_{h}(v)}=\langle v,h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

so that this interpretation of elements of
B
as elements of
B^{\prime}
is again consistent with the
duality pairing coming from
\mathcal{H}
.

{Backmatter}
